# CLAUDE.md

This file provides guidance to Claude Code (claude.ai/code) when working with code in this repository.

> **Expo has changed significantly.** Always read the versioned docs at https://docs.expo.dev/versions/v54.0.0/ before writing Expo-specific code.

## Project

**Patron** is a React Native (Expo Router v6) mobile commerce management app targeting small businesses in West Africa. UI and all labels are in French. Supports multiple currencies per business. Designed for iOS and Android (no web PWA target).

## Radical simplicity — the product mission (added 2026-09-22)

Referenced by name from several places in this codebase (`QuickCaptureSheet.tsx`'s own top comment among them) before this section actually existed — this is that section.

**The founder's own framing, stated directly:** Patron currently serves its *best* customers worst. A high-volume seller pays whatever friction the app has, many times a day — a 5-second delay recording a sale costs a once-a-week seller nothing; it costs someone doing 40 sales a day several real minutes, every day, forever. Real complaint already heard from merchants: "I can't pick up the phone" (too busy actually selling to also wrestle with the app), and "after the sale there's too much information" being asked for. The goal is not a feature-complete business app — it's a tool a busy shop owner opens, uses for the one thing they came to do, and closes, with nothing left over to think about. This is a deliberate rejection of engagement-optimizing design (streaks, feeds, "just one more thing") — Alpha and every other AI surface in this app exists to answer a question fast and get out of the way, not to hold a conversation.

**This is a working hypothesis, not yet a proven causal link, but it's the best one the data currently supports.** The Founder Dashboard's `growth_metrics` (see below) shows activation is healthy (64.3%) and, of businesses that ever record one sale, 74% sell again — acquisition and "getting someone to try it" are not the bottleneck. Week 1/Week 4 retention (4.65%/2.33% at last measurement) is where the funnel actually breaks: shops that try Patron mostly don't come back. The hypothesis this points to is that the app doesn't fail someone once in an obvious way — it costs them a little bit, repeatedly, in the screens they'd have to use every single day, and that adds up fastest for exactly the merchants doing the most volume (the ones most worth retaining).

**Practical consequence for prioritization: optimize the highest-*frequency* actions first, even at the expense of lower-frequency screens.** Ranked roughly by how often a real shop owner touches them: record a sale, record a credit, check today's total — then everything else (reports, équipe, paramètres) a distant second. A change that shaves real friction off one of the top three is worth more than a feature addition to something opened once a week.

**`QuickCaptureSheet.tsx` (added 2026-09) is the first concrete move under this mission**, not a one-off: recording a credit debt used to mean opening Vendre, building a cart (even though a credit debt isn't inventory), and stepping through a multi-field flow — real, measurable friction for what OkCredit/Khatabook (the reference category for this exact merchant segment — small shop owners in a low-connectivity market) treat as a two-field action: who, how much. The same lens — does this screen ask for more than the transaction strictly needs, and does it present a rarely-changed default (payment method, today's date, "cash") as a decision instead of just assuming it — is the one to apply anywhere else in the app, not just this one screen.

**"Reliable" is the enabling condition for "simple," not a separate, competing goal.** A fast-looking flow that silently hangs or drops data (the long, hard-won history of offline-queue/timeout bugs documented under "Offline queue" above) reads to a merchant as broken, not simple — the trust that makes a two-field form feel *safe* to use for real money is the same trust this codebase's whole reliability effort (`withTimeout()`, the cache-fallback sweep, `findUnprotectedFetchViolations()`) has been building. A simplification is not done until it's been checked against that same discipline, not just shipped because it looks cleaner.

**A real, admitted gap as of this writing: nobody has yet systematically mined *why* activated shops don't come back.** The retention number is measured; the cause isn't diagnosed. Two concrete, cheap sources of that signal that exist in this codebase already and appear not to have been mined for this purpose: (1) `support_conversations`/`support_messages` — real merchants already describe their actual frustrations to the founder in their own words, in French, unprompted; (2) a lightweight "why did you stop" prompt to a lapsed-but-previously-activated business would be a small, additive feature (new table + one client screen), cheap relative to guessing blind. Prioritize diagnosis before prescribing more fixes where the actual cause is still unconfirmed.

## Commands

```bash
# Dev server
npx expo start
npx expo start --ios
npx expo start --android

# Type-check
npx tsc --noEmit

# OTA update (JS-only changes — no native rebuild needed)
npx eas update --channel production --message "description"

# EAS build + submit to App Store / Play Store
eas build --platform ios --profile production
eas build --platform android --profile production
eas submit --platform ios --profile production
eas submit --platform android --profile production

# Full pre-merge gate: type-check + jest (mocked-Supabase unit tests) +
# consistency checks (palette-only colors, <Screen> as every screen root —
# see scripts/lib/consistency-checks.js) + eslint (react-hooks/rules-of-hooks
# only — see eslint.config.js and "Tabs layout" note below). Fast, hermetic,
# no Docker needed.
npm run check

# Integration tests — run the REAL submit_sale/cancel_sale/edit_sale/join_business
# Postgres functions (not a mocked supabase.rpc) against a local Supabase
# instance. Requires Docker (Colima works on older macOS where Docker
# Desktop's cask requires Sonoma+). One-time + per-run:
npm run test:db:start     # supabase start — local Postgres+Auth+PostgREST
npm run test:db:reset     # applies db/schema.sql + all db/migration_v*.sql in order
npm run test:integration  # jest --config jest.integration.config.js
```

Jest suite in `__tests__/` (offline queue, submit_sale, cart logic, role/session, error handling — no UI/component tests, all mock `supabase.rpc`). `__tests__/integration/` is separate: real RPC calls against a local Supabase instance — see "Integration tests" below. No linting config beyond the consistency-check script.

## UI components (`src/components/ui/`)

### Screen — required root for every screen

**Every screen file must use `<Screen>` as its root element, not `<SafeAreaView>` directly.**

```tsx
import { Screen } from '@/src/components/ui/Screen';

// Standard screen (top + bottom safe area):
return <Screen>...</Screen>;

// Tab-bar screen (top only — bottom handled by tab bar):
return <Screen tab>...</Screen>;

// Escape hatch for unusual edge needs:
return <Screen edges={['top']}>...</Screen>;
```

`Screen` applies `flex: 1`, `backgroundColor: palette.background`, and the correct safe area edges automatically. Using raw `<SafeAreaView>` as a screen root is a violation — it's easy to forget `edges` and content ends up behind the notch or home indicator. `SafeAreaView` is still allowed inside `<Modal>` components (for modal sheet inner wrappers).

This rule (and the "never hardcode hex" rule under Theme below) is enforced by `scripts/lib/consistency-checks.js`, run via `npm run check` — an import-based check for `SafeAreaView` usage (not a same-line grep, so legitimate Modal-nested usages aren't flagged) and a hex-literal grep over `app/`, `src/`, `stores/`.

`scripts/daily-rapport.js` (the GitHub Actions daily email, `.github/workflows/daily-rapport.yml`) used to run its own inline version of the SafeAreaView check: grep every `SafeAreaView` line, then drop lines containing the literal text `Modal`. Since `<Modal>` is virtually always several lines above `<SafeAreaView>`, not on the same line, that filter almost never matched — every legitimate Modal-nested `SafeAreaView` (imports, open tag, close tag) was being reported as a violation, ~64 false positives a day. It now imports the same `scripts/lib/consistency-checks.js` used by `npm run check`, so both surfaces agree and the report reflects real violations (0, currently) instead of noise.

**The "Rapport quotidien" GitHub Actions workflow silently failed on every single scheduled run for at least 7 weeks (2026-07-15 through 2026-09-02, confirmed 100/100 failures via `gh run list`) before being caught, fixed 2026-09-03.** `.github/workflows/daily-rapport.yml` passes `RESEND_API_KEY: ${{ secrets.RESEND_API_KEY }}` into `node scripts/daily-rapport.js` — but `gh api repos/kolilink/Patron/actions/secrets` showed `total_count: 0`: no GitHub Actions repository secret was ever set, so the env var was always empty and the script's own `if (!RESEND_KEY)` guard exited 1 every time before sending anything. This is the exact same failure class already documented under "PostHog analytics" above (a value that exists in one secret store but was never propagated to the one that actually needs it at runtime) — here the two stores are GitHub Actions repo secrets vs. Supabase Edge Function secrets (`supabase secrets list` does show a `RESEND_API_KEY` entry, but that's a completely separate store consumed only by `send-report-email`/`send-email-otp`/etc., and GitHub Actions has no access to it). Fixed with `gh secret set RESEND_API_KEY --repo kolilink/Patron`, verified with a manual `gh workflow run` + `gh run watch` before trusting it, same "smoke-test the real call, don't just trust that config looks right" posture as the `--no-verify-jwt` cron fix above. **Any secret a GitHub Actions workflow reads via `${{ secrets.* }}` must be set as a repo (or org/environment) secret explicitly — having the same-named value in Supabase, `.env`, or anywhere else does not make it visible to Actions.** `gh api repos/<owner>/<repo>/actions/secrets` (returns names only, `total_count` is the fast check) is the way to confirm a workflow's required secrets actually exist, before assuming a scheduled workflow that "looks configured" is actually running.

**The "Patron — Rapport Quotidien Combiné" Claude cloud routine (a `/schedule`-created routine, not a GitHub Action — different mechanism entirely) was also silently failing every day, for a third, different reason (found and fixed 2026-09-03, same investigation as the GitHub Actions fix above).** This routine generated the code-quality/migration-drift half of the report via an LLM, then `curl POST`ed it to `send-report-email` (the relay that adds the reconciliation half — 78 DB checks, business/shop counts — server-side and sends via Resend). Every run hit `curl: (56) CONNECT tunnel failed, response 403` — Claude's cloud-sandbox egress proxy denies outbound connections to any domain not on its allowlist (npm, PyPI, Anthropic's own API, etc.), and `*.supabase.co` was never on it. **Unlike the GitHub Actions failure, this one never surfaced as a "run failed" notification** — the routine catches the blocked POST, sends a `PushNotification` instead, and reports `ROUTINE_RUN_STATUS_SUCCEEDED` to Claude's scheduler regardless, since it completed without crashing. A routine "succeeding" on the platform's own dashboard is not evidence it delivered anything — check what it actually *did* (`RemoteTrigger` `list_runs` + `get_run_log`) before trusting that a scheduled routine's silence means it's working. There is no tool/API exposed to adjust that cloud-sandbox network allowlist, so the fix was to stop depending on it: `scripts/daily-report-combined.js` + `.github/workflows/daily-report-combined.yml` reimplement the same code-quality checks (TypeScript, hardcoded hex, direct writes to `sale_orders`/`so_lines`/`stock_moves`/`memberships` bypassing RPC, a raw-`parseFloat` review list) and a lightweight migration-drift summary (new migrations this week + their own header-comment block, rather than an LLM judgment call a deterministic script can't replicate) as a plain Node script, then POST to the same relay via `REPORT_RELAY_SECRET` — GitHub Actions has unrestricted outbound access, so this has no dependency on the cloud-sandbox policy at all. PostHog analytics/device sections (the old routine's D/E) are left as an explicit "not available" placeholder rather than faking a check — they need a PostHog personal API key this workflow doesn't have, and were already broken in the cloud routine too (`get_run_log` showed "PostHog's MCP connector isn't authorized in this session" on every scheduled run — the MCP connection is authorized for interactive use but not for a non-interactive scheduled fire). **Two GitHub Actions workflows now independently email the founder daily** (`daily-rapport.yml`'s code-quality-only report, and this combined one) — deliberately not merged into one, since the founder asked to fix each as its own thing, not consolidate them.

### Form sheets — Android keyboard flicker (`src/components/ui/FormSheet.tsx`, fixed 2026-07-28)

A merchant's video showed the software keyboard flashing open/closed repeatedly while typing a product name in "Nouveau produit" (catalogue.tsx), on Android, battery fully charged, reproducible on demand. Root cause: `app.json`'s `edgeToEdgeEnabled: true` (live since the very first commit) means the whole app draws behind the system status/nav bars — but React Native's `<Modal>` opens as its own **separate native Android window**, and that window does not inherit the main window's edge-to-edge treatment unless `statusBarTranslucent`/`navigationBarTranslucent` are explicitly set on it (neither was, anywhere in the app). The mismatch — main window edge-to-edge, Modal window not — made the Modal's own `windowSoftInputMode` keyboard-pan handling race against inconsistent inset delivery, and that race is what surfaced as visible flicker. It's a real, documented RN/Android bug class ([react-native-modal#816](https://github.com/react-native-modal/react-native-modal/issues/816), [expo#38711](https://github.com/expo/expo/issues/38711)), not a device-specific fluke — every screen with a `<Modal>` containing a keyboard field was equally exposed, just rarely enough (this exact screen touched once per product, on whichever Android device happened to be slow enough for the race to become visible) that only one had ever been reported.

`FormSheet` is the fix: a shared component that always sets both props on its `<Modal>`, so every current and future form-style screen gets them for free instead of every screen author having to remember to set them individually. It replaced ~30 individual `<Modal>`+`SafeAreaView`+header+`ScrollView`+footer blocks across ~20 files. Two things are load-bearing, not incidental:
- Forcing `statusBarTranslucent` means the Modal's content now draws *behind* the status bar, so the header needs its own top safe-area inset on Android (previously not needed, since the un-translucent Modal already avoided the status bar on its own) — `FormSheet` uses `edges: ['top','bottom']` on Android, `['bottom']` on iOS (whose native page-sheet chrome already handles top).
- `navigationBarTranslucent` requires `statusBarTranslucent` to also be `true` or RN silently misbehaves (a dev warning fires from RN's own `Modal.js`) — always set both together, never just one.

Not every Modal fits `FormSheet`'s shape (full-screen, header + scroll + footer). Two real exceptions, handled by adding the same two props directly to a bespoke `<Modal>` instead: `src/components/BusinessDrawer.tsx` (a gesture-driven slide-in-from-left side panel, not a slide-up sheet) and every small backdrop+rounded-panel action sheet (`AppSheet`'s own shape, e.g. the dashboard's withdraw sheet, `vendre.tsx`'s variant picker, `equipe/index.tsx`'s payout sheet) — these keep their custom presentation but still need the props if they contain a keyboard field, since the underlying Android window mismatch is identical regardless of chrome.

**Enforced by `scripts/lib/consistency-checks.js`'s `findRawModalWithTextInputViolations()`**, wired into both `npm run check` and `__tests__/consistency-checks.test.ts`: any `<Modal>` sharing a component function with a keyboard field (`<TextInput>`, `<Input>`, or `<PhoneInput>` — not `<DatePickerField>`, which opens a native date picker, never the software keyboard) must itself carry `statusBarTranslucent` — enforcing the actual prop invariant, not literally "must use FormSheet," so the two legitimate bespoke cases above pass without an exemption list. Building this check surfaced three of its own bugs before it was trustworthy, each found by re-verifying a fix that should have cleared and didn't: (1) the original brace-index used `line.indexOf('{', ...)`, which finds a destructured param's own `{` instead of the function body's — fixed by taking the index from the end of the regex match instead; (2) the function-boundary regex was line-anchored and silently skipped any component whose parameter list spans multiple lines (`vendre.tsx`'s `PaymentModal` — arguably the single highest-traffic modal in the app — went completely unchecked because of this); (3) the tag-matching regex ended at the first `>`, which breaks on both an arrow-function prop (`onRequestClose={() => ...}`) and a `//` comment mentioning a JSX tag by name (this file's own comment on `BusinessDrawer`'s Modal, which says `<FormSheet>`, tripped it) — both are now matched as atomic units so their `>` is never mistaken for the tag's close. A regex-based check reporting "clean" is not proof of anything until you've tried it against a case it should catch and confirmed it actually catches it.

**`FormSheet`'s `footer` prop has no built-in styling of its own — every screen supplies its own footer `View` style, which means the "hard border vs. soft shadow" fix already made once (catalogue.tsx's product form) doesn't propagate anywhere else on its own.** Found again in `app/(app)/fournisseurs/index.tsx` and `[id].tsx`'s "Nouvelle commande"/PO forms (2026-08-25): both still used `borderTopWidth: 1, borderTopColor: p.border` on the footer, which reads as a flat rectangle sitting behind the submit button rather than part of the sheet — the same complaint that motivated catalogue.tsx's fix. There is no shared "form footer" style token to inherit from; `mfooter`/`modalFooter` are copy-pasted per screen. Fixed both to the same `backgroundColor: p.background, ...shadow.md, shadowOffset: { width: 0, height: -2 }` treatment, and added `insets.bottom`-aware padding on the "Nouvelle commande" footer specifically (neither had it — `FormSheet`'s own `SafeAreaView` doesn't cover `footer`, which renders as its sibling). Same session: their "Total"-style summary rows (`totalRow`, a `flexDirection: 'row', justifyContent: 'space-between'` pairing a label with an `amountLarge` figure) were changed from a row to a vertical stack with `numberOfLines={1}`/`adjustsFontSizeToFit` on the amount — a large order total has no upper bound on digit count, and a side-by-side row has no graceful way to fit both once the number runs long. **Any new form-sheet footer or label+amount summary row should be checked against these two now-established patterns instead of copy-pasting whatever the nearest existing screen happens to have** — nothing enforces this automatically the way `findRawModalWithTextInputViolations()` enforces the Modal-prop invariant above.

### Real elevation + `Pill` (added 2026-09-02) — `Card.tsx`'s `elevated` prop was dead code

A design audit (screenshots of Cash App/Robinhood/Revolut vs. this app) found the "flat/dead" feel wasn't missing polish everywhere — `src/theme`'s `shadow` tokens and `surfaceElevated` palette entry already existed, but `Card`'s `elevated` prop only ever added `shadow.md`; the background stayed hardcoded to `p.surface`, so `surfaceElevated` was never actually read anywhere. Fixed: `elevated` now also sets `backgroundColor: p.surfaceElevated` (a no-op in light mode, where the two are intentionally equal — light-mode depth reads via shadow, not a lighter fill; it lightens the fill correctly in dark mode). `elevated` stays **opt-in**, reserved for one focal card per screen (dashboard hero cards, `plus.tsx`'s menu rows) — flipping the default would touch every dense list row app-wide at once with no way to scope or revert it incrementally.

New `src/components/ui/Pill.tsx` centralizes the "loud, rare color" pattern from the same audit: `variant="soft"` (default) is the existing tinted-badge look (stock/status pills); `variant="solid"` (white text on a full color fill) is new and deliberately kept to one file so it stays rare by construction — reach for it only where a real directional/state signal exists (currently: the dashboard's today-vs-yesterday revenue delta, `app/(app)/(tabs)/index.tsx`). Solid pills read in direct sunlight where tinted text doesn't, which matters for this audience (shop owners frequently checking the app outdoors). `Pill`'s `tone` type is `'success' | 'warning'` only — no `'danger'` — structurally enforcing the standing no-red-in-merchant-UI rule rather than relying on every call site remembering it.

Also fixed while doing this pass (opportunistic, not the main change): the dashboard's "À racheter" (low-stock) KPI card was passing `accent={palette.danger}` — a real red, violating the app's own no-red rule, sitting right next to a sibling card that correctly used `palette.warning`. Same violation found and fixed in `catalogue.tsx`'s live profit-margin preview and archived-product profit color (both were `stats.profit >= 0 ? success : danger`). **Not fully swept**: `grep -rl "palette.danger" app/ src/ stores/` (excluding the founder-only dashboard, which has a deliberate scoped exception — see below) still turns up hits in `discussions.tsx`, `fournisseurs/*`, `ventes/index.tsx`, `vendre.tsx`, `support/index.tsx`, `equipe/index.tsx`, `alpha/index.tsx`, `parametres/index.tsx` — some of these are likely legitimate (e.g. `Button`'s `variant="danger"` for a real destructive action like "Se déconnecter"), some may not be; each needs a human judgment call per call site, not a blanket find-replace, so this is flagged as a follow-up rather than fixed here.

`app/(app)/(tabs)/_layout.tsx`'s bottom tab bar and `plus.tsx`'s menu rows (the "Ventes & Clients"/"Argent"/"Achats" settings list) got the same upward-offset `shadow.md` trick already used for sticky footers elsewhere (`fournisseurs/*`, `catalogue.tsx`) — token-only changes, no layout rework. `BusinessDrawer.tsx` (the lateral business-switcher) already had a real, fairly strong shadow (`shadowOpacity: 0.18, elevation: 12`) — an initial pass assumed it needed the same fix and was wrong; always verify a component's actual current styles before assuming a class of bug applies to it. `palette.primary`'s existing governance note (added in this pass, next to `paletteLight.primary` in `colors.ts`): reserved for CTA fills, active/selected states, and real brand moments — not a generic price/link/icon tint. 181 existing call sites (107 of them decorative text/icon color) were **not** batch-migrated — no mechanical way to separate "real affordance" from "decorative tint" per site; clean up only opportunistically when a screen is already being touched for another reason.

**Dashboard's "Parler avec Alpha…" floating pill removed entirely (2026-09-02), not just restyled.** It had a slow-rotating gradient glow ring around the border — exactly the "loud but constant" pattern the redesign above argues against, and it permanently ate vertical space on the one screen meant to lead with the day's revenue number. Alpha's only dashboard entry point is now the plain, uncolored "A" header icon that already existed as a secondary entry — a real, accepted tradeoff (the icon is a quieter "back to my conversation" affordance, not an inviting "ask something new" prompt); revisit if Alpha engagement from Accueil drops.

**List-row data density pass (2026-09-02), comparing Robinhood/Cash App's list rows against Catalogue/Clients/Fournisseurs/Commandes.** Finding: Robinhood pairs every row with a trend signal (color, sparkline) because a stock's whole point is "is it moving" — Patron's data isn't like that, so the transferable principle is narrower: pair a number with a real state signal where one actually exists, not decorate everything. `Clients` already did this well (debt amount, due-date urgency color, inline WhatsApp action) and was left alone. Two real, scoped fixes made:
- `fournisseurs/index.tsx`'s Commandes (PO) list status pill now routes `recu`/`recu_partiel` through the shared `Pill` (they're real signals — done/needs-attention) via a new `OrderStatusPill` helper; `brouillon`/`envoye`/`annule` deliberately keep their own plain tinted-label treatment (unchanged) rather than being force-fit into `Pill`'s restricted `success|warning` tone type — they're neutral workflow-stage labels, not a signal to react to.
- Fournisseur rows gained a debt-age line ("Depuis N jours", amber past `DEBT_AGE_WARNING_DAYS = 14`) — **`supplier_debts` has no `due_date` column** (unlike `sale_orders.due_date` for client credit), so this tracks age-since-recorded instead of a real due date; it's a same-spirit urgency signal, not a literal match of anything Clients displays (Clients computes an equivalent `daysOldestDebt` today but only uses it to *sort* the "doivent" filter, never shows it as row text — so this is a new, disclosed UI pattern, not a port of an existing one).

**Catalogue was flagged as the weakest of the four** (plain letter-badge avatar, no per-row "is this selling" signal, and — the real, most-requested gap — no product photos at all) but deliberately left untouched this pass. Product photos are a real feature, not a style tweak: needs a storage bucket, an upload UI (camera/gallery), a schema column, and the same offline-created-product handling this app already built for transaction-proof and chat images (can't upload while offline, attaches later) — scope it as its own project rather than folding it into a polish pass.

**Explicitly not done, and why**: full-bleed per-tab color/gradient backgrounds (Revolut/Cash App style) were considered and rejected — this app has one obsessively-checked screen (Accueil), not several competing product areas, so the wayfinding rationale those apps have doesn't transfer; it would also cost more (GPU load on low-end Android, doubled dark-mode QA) than it's worth for a weaker fit. A detached floating-pill tab bar (Robinhood-style) was *also* rejected in this same pass for the same reason — but was requested again and actually built the same week; see "Floating tab bar" under Architecture below for what that took.

## Architecture

### Routing (Expo Router v6 file-based)

```
app/
  _layout.tsx           — Root layout: initializes auth + SQLite, hides splash
  index.tsx             — Redirects to (welcome) or (app) based on session
  (welcome)/            — Unauthenticated: index, connexion, creer, rejoindre
  (app)/
    _layout.tsx         — Guards: redirects to /(welcome)/ if no session. Mounts SyncBanner
    (tabs)/             — Bottom tabs: Accueil, Catalogue, Vendre, Plus
    onboarding/         — Create or join a business after first login
    ventes/             — Sales history + detail
    depenses/           — Expense management
    equipe/             — Team management
    fournisseurs/       — Supplier list + detail ([id].tsx)
    clients/            — Customer list + ledger ([name].tsx)
    credits/            — Credit tracking
    rapports/           — Reports
    parametres/         — Settings, leave business, delete account, privacy links
    discussions.tsx     — Boutique private chat room
    marche/             — Le Marché community forum (index + [id].tsx post detail)
    support/            — Merchant → founder support chat (single thread per business)
    support-inbox/      — Founder-only: all open support threads across every business
```

`investisseur` role sees only the Accueil and Plus tabs — Catalogue and Vendre use `href: null` to hide them. Investisseurs have **read-only** access to sale_orders, expenses, and products (added v38).

**Tabs layout — Rules-of-Hooks crash on logout (fixed 2026-07-16).** `(tabs)/_layout.tsx` had `if (!session?.activeBusiness) return null;` sitting *above* `useChatStore()` and its `loadChat` `useEffect` — meaning those two hooks only ran while logged in (8 hooks/render), and were skipped entirely (6 hooks/render) the instant `logout()` nulled the session and this still-mounted layout re-rendered before `(app)/_layout.tsx`'s own redirect could unmount it. React's hook-count invariant broke and threw ("Rendered fewer hooks than expected"), crashing the whole tree on every tap of "Se déconnecter" in Paramètres. The guard itself predates the hooks (harmless when added); the two hooks were added underneath it later (commit `88dc531f`) without noticing the early return above them. Fixed by hoisting both above the guard and null-guarding their bodies (`session?.activeBusiness?.id` etc.) — all hooks now run unconditionally every render, only the JSX/redirect branches on session state. **This is now guarded by `eslint.config.js`** (`react-hooks/rules-of-hooks`, the only rule enabled — `exhaustive-deps` is deliberately off, since 100+ effects in this codebase are intentionally mount-only and would just bury the one signal this setup exists for), wired into `npm run check`. Any component with an early return must have **all** its hooks above that return, no exceptions — this is the specific shape (hooks added below a pre-existing guard) that slips past review because the diff alone looks fine in isolation.

**This hooks crash was masking a second, independent native crash on logout — see "Native IAP paywall" below for the RevenueCat `logOut()` fix.** Because the hooks bug threw synchronously and immediately on every logout tap, the app never lived long enough to reach the async `logoutPurchases()` call further down `stores/auth.ts`'s `logout()` — so that bug had zero observed impact until this one was fixed, at which point logout crashed again, differently, on the very next OTA. Lesson: fixing a crash that fires early in a sequence can unmask a second bug further along the same path that was never actually exercised before — don't assume a sequence is clean just because the first fix resolves the reported symptom; retest the full flow, not just "does it still crash."

### Floating tab bar (`(tabs)/_layout.tsx` + `Screen.tsx`, added 2026-09-02) — genuinely `position: 'absolute'`, not a flush bar with rounded corners

Requested directly (Robinhood/Cash App/Revolut comparison) after the same-week color/elevation redesign above. Explicitly the harder of two options that were laid out — a flush bar with blur was the safer alternative, rejected in favor of the real thing.

**Why this is riskier than everything else in that redesign, and what it actually touches.** A flush tab bar reserves its own height in normal flex layout — every sibling screen's content area is automatically shrunk to make room for it, for free. A `position: 'absolute'` tab bar reserves nothing; it floats on top, and every screen underneath now extends the full height of its container, including the space the bar used to claim. That ripples into every place something was positioned assuming the old bar's flex space existed:
- **`src/theme/spacing.ts`** gets three new constants — `FLOATING_TAB_BAR_HEIGHT` (60), `FLOATING_TAB_BAR_GAP` (12, between the pill and the safe-area inset), `FLOATING_TAB_BAR_CLEARANCE` (their sum + a little breathing room) — one shared source of truth so the bar's own position math and every consumer's clearance math can't drift apart independently.
- **`Screen.tsx`'s `tab` prop** used to mean `edges: ['top']` only (bottom handled by the old bar). Now it's `edges: ['top', 'bottom']` (same as a normal screen — the real OS safe-area inset has to be reserved again, since nothing else claims it) **plus** an explicit `paddingBottom: FLOATING_TAB_BAR_CLEARANCE` for the pill's own footprint on top of that.
- **Absolutely-positioned bottom-anchored elements on a tab screen do NOT inherit that padding.** This is React Native's well-documented behavior, not a bug to work around: padding on a parent only affects normal-flow (relative/static) children, never `position: 'absolute'` children — so `Screen`'s new `paddingBottom` correctly shrinks a `ScrollView`/`FlatList`'s available height (ordinary flex child, gets the benefit for free) but does **nothing** for something like `vendre.tsx`'s cart checkout bar, which is its own `position: 'absolute', bottom: 0` sibling. Every such element needs `FLOATING_TAB_BAR_CLEARANCE` added to its own bottom offset by hand: `vendre.tsx`'s `cartPanel` (was `bottom: 0`) and both `vendre.tsx`'s and `catalogue.tsx`'s FAB (`fabContainer`, was `bottom: 194` — a value already hand-tuned against the old bar's flex space, so the fix is `194 + FLOATING_TAB_BAR_CLEARANCE`, not a fresh number). Confirmed via `grep -rn "<Screen tab"` that only these four screens (`index.tsx`, `catalogue.tsx`, `vendre.tsx` ×2 branches, `plus.tsx`) are in scope — every other screen (`ventes/`, `depenses/`, `equipe/`, `fournisseurs/`, `clients/`) lives outside the `(tabs)/` group entirely and never renders alongside this bar.
- **Translucency was tried, then deliberately dropped the same day** — see the fourth follow-up below for why and what replaced it. `expo-blur` is no longer imported by this file at all.
- The `FLOATING_TAB_BAR_CLEARANCE` numbers (60/12/12) are a reasoned first pass, not device-verified — if the pill visually collides with cart bars/FABs or leaves too much/too little air below scrollable content on a real phone, tune the constants in `spacing.ts` (one place) rather than chasing it per-screen.

None of this is native/config-level — no `app.json`/`package.json` change — so it's OTA-safe by the `runtimeVersion: 'fingerprint'` policy's own rules.

**Follow-up fix, same day, from a real device screenshot: the first version read as "moved up + small radius," not actually glassy or floating.** Two concrete misses, not vague polish: (1) the tint overlay on top of the blur was 0.7/0.55 opacity — high enough that the blur underneath was contributing nothing visible, so the bar looked like a plain opaque rounded rectangle; lowered to 0.42/0.38 and added a subtle light-edge border (`rgba(255,255,255,0.6)`/`0.14`) for the glass-edge definition that was otherwise missing entirely. (2) There was no active-tab indicator at all beyond icon color — every reference app's floating nav uses a background pill behind the selected icon, and this had nothing analogous. New `TabIconChip` wraps `HomeTabIcon` and the generic `tabIcon()` helper (Catalogue, Vendre) in a capsule (fill color revised again in the fourth follow-up below) that only appears when focused. **Deliberately not applied to `ProfileTabIcon`** — it already has its own focus treatment (opacity dimming on an already-circular avatar); wrapping that in a second pill would nest two circles instead of reading as one coherent selected-state.

**Second follow-up, same day, from a second real device screenshot: the selected-state shape itself was wrong, not just under-tuned.** `TabIconChip`'s first version was `minWidth: 48, height: 36` — an oval sized like a label+icon pill, when these tabs are icon-only and every reference app uses a tight, centered **circle** for an icon-only selected state, not a stretched pill. Rebuilt around one shared `TAB_CIRCLE_SIZE = 44` (Apple HIG's minimum tap-target size — a real, citable number, not picked by eye) used for **both** `TabIconChip`'s circle and `ProfileTabIcon`'s avatar circle (previously its own independently-derived `TAB_ICON_SIZE + 6 = 32`, one more incidental size nobody had reconciled against the others) — all four tab items now share one identical 44px footprint. `TAB_ICON_SIZE` (the glyph size inside the circle) dropped 26→22, landing it at roughly half the circle's diameter — the same glyph-to-circle proportion the reference apps use, instead of a glyph that nearly filled its own bounding box with almost no breathing room.

**Third follow-up, same day: the circle itself was right, but sat off-center within the bar.** `@react-navigation/bottom-tabs` reserves vertical space for a label under the icon even with `tabBarShowLabel: false` — its default tab-item layout doesn't automatically re-center when the label disappears, so `TabIconChip`'s 44px circle was centered within that taller implicit box, not within the bar's real 60px height, reading as "too low." Fixed with an explicit `tabBarItemStyle: { height: FLOATING_TAB_BAR_HEIGHT, paddingTop: 0, paddingBottom: 0, justifyContent: 'center', alignItems: 'center' }` in `screenOptions` — pins every tab button to the bar's actual height and centers on both axes, overriding the library's own label-shaped default rather than fighting it with more padding tweaks.

**Fourth follow-up, same day: dropped blur entirely, and fixed a real color-governance regression, not just cosmetics.** Two separate, concrete problems, both confirmed by directly comparing against Robinhood screenshots rather than going on vibes:
1. **The blur/tint canvas was structurally wrong for this app, not under-tuned.** On a flat, mostly monochrome-gray screen (Accueil has none of the photos/gradients a blur needs to visibly distort), no amount of opacity tuning made it read as real glass — it sat in an undefined middle state, neither a confident opaque pill nor a legible frosted one. Compounding it: the border color (`rgba(255,255,255,0.6)` in light mode) was a glassmorphism convention borrowed from dark/colorful backdrops, where a bright translucent edge creates a highlight — on this app's light gray background it did nothing, or read as a faint, unexplained halo. Fixed by dropping `expo-blur`/`tabBarBackground`/`FloatingTabBarBackground` entirely and setting `tabBarStyle.backgroundColor: palette.surfaceElevated` directly — a clean opaque pill with a real shadow, which is what Robinhood's own bar actually amounts to once you account for it blending into a similarly light page. This also retroactively resolves the previously-flagged "transparent-background shadow on Android" risk — there's no transparency left to be uncertain about.
2. **The active tab's icon color (`palette.primary`, bright indigo) was the same "brand color reached for out of habit, not as a real signal" problem this session's redesign fixed in 100+ other places, put right back here.** Reference apps' nav bars are monochrome — active is a solid dark glyph, inactive is lighter gray, and color never touches nav chrome at all; the *selected circle* is what's supposed to carry "this is active," not the icon's hue. Changed `tabBarActiveTintColor` to `palette.textPrimary`, and `TabIconChip`'s focused fill from `palette.primaryLight` to the neutral `palette.border` (a soft gray highlight, not a brand-colored one) — matching how Robinhood's own selected-tab circle is gray, never colored.

**Fifth follow-up, same day: `tabBarItemStyle` centering (third follow-up above) wasn't the whole fix — a second, separate library default was still pushing the icon high.** Reported as "the icons feel off, not even integrated with the bar" from a real device screenshot, after the third follow-up had already shipped. Root cause: `@react-navigation/bottom-tabs` gives the icon's own wrapper view a default `marginBottom` (reserved for a label slot) that's independent of `tabBarItemStyle` — centering the outer button (what the third follow-up fixed) doesn't touch this inner wrapper's own margin, so the icon still rendered high inside an already-centered box. `tabBarItemStyle`'s `justifyContent:/alignItems: 'center'` and this inner margin are two separate defaults that both had to be zeroed independently; fixing only the outer one was a real, verifiable partial fix, not a wrong one. Added `tabBarIconStyle: { margin: 0, width: TAB_CIRCLE_SIZE, height: TAB_CIRCLE_SIZE }` to `screenOptions` to close the gap. Any future adjustment to icon vertical position in this bar should check both `tabBarItemStyle` and `tabBarIconStyle` — a fix to only one has already shipped once and turned out incomplete.

**Sixth follow-up, same day: `ProfileTabIcon` removed entirely; the profile tab is now a plain person icon, requested directly for cross-account uniformity.** It used to render a colored circle with the signed-in user's initial (role-tinted background, opacity-dimmed when unfocused) — a one-off treatment that also needed its own `TAB_CIRCLE_SIZE` alignment hack to match the other three tabs' footprint. Replaced with `tabBarIcon: tabIcon('person-outline', 'person')` — the exact same generic helper Catalogue/Vendre already use, now wrapped in the same `TabIconChip` as every other tab, matching Robinhood's own profile tab (a generic icon, not a per-user avatar) and removing the alignment special-case at the same time. **Tapping the tab still opens the same Plus/profile screen, completely unchanged** — only the tab bar glyph changed; `plus.tsx`'s own in-screen profile card (name, real avatar, role badge) is a separate component and still shows the real signed-in user. `ROLE_COLORS`/`ROLE_COLORS_DARK`/`generateFallbackName` imports were dropped from this file — no longer used anywhere in it.

**Also bumped `FLOATING_TAB_BAR_HEIGHT` 60→64 in the same pass** (`src/theme/spacing.ts`) — reported against a Robinhood comparison that the selected circle read as "not implied within the bar," too close to filling it edge-to-edge. **Reversed one follow-up later (below) — this was the wrong lever.** Growing the bar to fit a fixed 44px circle just made the whole pill read as bulkier/oversized, a separate complaint that arrived immediately after.

**Seventh follow-up, same day: bar height correction — the previous fix solved the wrong problem.** "Too large/long, look at Robinhood, it just fits" plus "the selector fills all of it" arrived together, and both point at the same root cause: the sixth follow-up's 60→64 bump kept the circle fixed at 44px and grew the *bar* around it, when the actual issue was the circle-to-bar ratio, not the bar's absolute size. Reversed course: `FLOATING_TAB_BAR_HEIGHT` 64→**56** (`src/theme/spacing.ts`) and `TAB_CIRCLE_SIZE` 44→**36** (`(tabs)/_layout.tsx`) together — a slimmer bar paired with a smaller, more clearly-inset circle, instead of an oversized bar built to fit a circle that was never resized. `TAB_ICON_SIZE` followed proportionally, 22→20, keeping roughly the same ~1:2 glyph-to-circle ratio established earlier. **The Apple HIG tap-target reasoning for 44px doesn't actually break by shrinking the drawn circle** — `tabBarItemStyle` makes the *whole tab button* (the bar's full height × roughly a third of its width) the real tappable region regardless of how small the visual circle inside it is, so the citable HIG justification was about the tap target, not the decorative shape, and doesn't constrain this number the way it first seemed to. Lesson for this component specifically: when a selected-state element looks disproportionate, check both halves of the ratio (the container and the content) before assuming only one of them needs to move — the sixth follow-up moved only the container and made a different problem worse.

**Eighth follow-up, same day: the background circle itself — its existence, not its size or color — was the actual mistake, four follow-ups in.** After sizing/color/shape/proportion were each individually fixed and still reported as "still different," re-checked the original premise directly against every Robinhood screenshot collected during this conversation, not just the one crypto screen the "background pill behind the active icon" idea first came from — and found no visible background chip behind their active tab in any of the others. Their selected state is icon color alone (solid dark vs. lighter gray), full stop. `TabIconChip` no longer draws a background in either state — it's kept only as a shared sizing/centering wrapper (`width/height: TAB_CIRCLE_SIZE`, no `focused` prop, no `backgroundColor`) so all four tabs still share one identical footprint for alignment purposes. Selection is now communicated exactly one way: `tabBarActiveTintColor`/`tabBarInactiveTintColor` (already neutral dark vs. gray, set in the fourth follow-up). **Lesson bigger than this one component**: four consecutive rounds of tuning a circle's size, color, and proportions all failed to resolve "still different" because none of them questioned whether the circle should exist at all — when repeated parameter tuning on the same element keeps missing, the next move is re-verifying the underlying premise against the original reference, not tuning the same parameters harder.

**Ninth follow-up, same day: the eighth follow-up's premise was itself wrong — reversed back, plus two more concrete, measured misses fixed in the same pass.** A clean, unobscured screenshot of Robinhood's search tab (unlike the earlier ones, which were either covered by a toast or too low-contrast to tell) settled it: the active icon *does* sit on a small gray rounded-rect background — it's just tight around the icon, not stretched to fill its tab slot's full width, which is what made it invisible in the lower-contrast screenshots the eighth follow-up drew its conclusion from. So the background-chip premise wasn't wrong; only its proportions were, the same class of mistake as the earlier size/color rounds — removing the element outright was an overcorrection. `TabIconChip` regained its `focused` prop and `palette.border` background, unchanged from before the eighth follow-up.

Two more, independently reported in the same message: (1) **"Too long on the Y axis, doesn't take the whole screen height like Robinhood's doesn't"** — `FLOATING_TAB_BAR_HEIGHT` 56→**52** (`src/theme/spacing.ts`), landing closer to a classic ~49-50pt compact tab bar core rather than a taller "premium pill" shape. (2) **"Too wide on the X axis, goes too far left and right"** — the bar's `left`/`right` inset (`tabBarStyle` in `(tabs)/_layout.tsx`) widened from `spacing[4]` (16) to `spacing[7]` (28), giving it real margin from the screen edges instead of nearly spanning full width. Neither of these two had been touched in any earlier follow-up — every prior round adjusted the circle or the bar's *fill*, never its outer footprint.

**Tenth follow-up, same day: "reduce the space between the icons" and the ninth follow-up's own X-axis fix are the same lever, not two separate ones.** React Navigation gives each of the 4 tabs an equal flex share of `tabBarStyle`'s own width — there's no independent "gap between icons" control to reach for; the only way to bring icons closer together is to make the bar itself narrower, which is exactly what the reporter predicted ("when you reduce icon spacing, there'll be leftover space on the sides — remove that too"), correctly identifying both symptoms as one root cause before I did. `spacing[7]` (28) → **`spacing[14]` (56)** on the same `left`/`right` inset — the ninth follow-up's widening was correct in direction but too small a step once "the icons themselves need to sit closer together" was the actual ask, not just "a bit more edge margin."

**Eleventh follow-up, same day: the tenth follow-up's mechanism was structurally wrong, not just insufficiently aggressive.** `left`/`right` insets (used by every prior width-related follow-up, the tenth included) always stretch the bar to fill whatever space is left between those two edges — there is no amount of widening that inset which produces a bar that *stops* growing with the screen and instead hugs its own content. Reported plainly, more than once, as the bar still "going all the way right and left" after the tenth follow-up shipped. Replaced the mechanism entirely: `tabBarStyle` now sets a fixed `width: TAB_BAR_WIDTH` and a computed `left: (windowWidth - TAB_BAR_WIDTH) / 2` (via `useWindowDimensions`) instead of `left`+`right` insets — genuinely content-sized and centered, not screen-relative. `TAB_BAR_WIDTH` is derived from real numbers, not picked by eye: `4 × TAB_CIRCLE_SIZE (36) + 3 × spacing[6] (24, the gap between adjacent icons) + 2 × spacing[4] (16, this app's standard screen-edge padding value everywhere else)` = 248. **Lesson**: this is the second time in this component's history that several rounds of tuning a *value* (spacing[4]→7→14) never could have worked, because the *mechanism* (edge insets on an otherwise-stretching container) structurally couldn't produce the requested result at any value — same shape of miss as the eighth/ninth follow-ups' circle-existence question. When a parameter has been pushed several times in the same direction and the complaint doesn't change, check whether the mechanism itself can reach the target state at all before pushing the number further.

**Twelfth follow-up, same day: the eleventh follow-up's own centering computation rendered off-center-left in practice.** `left: (windowWidth - TAB_BAR_WIDTH) / 2` via `useWindowDimensions()` is correct in principle, but reported as visibly sitting toward the left rather than centered — most likely `useWindowDimensions()`'s reported width not matching the actual width Yoga lays this specific view out against (this app is edge-to-edge on Android and this is a `position: 'absolute'` view nested inside a tab navigator, either of which could plausibly introduce a gap between "JS-reported window width" and "real native parent width" — not confirmed on-device, just the most likely explanation available). Replaced with a computation that can't have this class of mismatch at all: `left: '50%', marginLeft: -TAB_BAR_WIDTH / 2` — the standard fixed-width-centering technique, resolved by the native layout engine directly against this view's real parent width at layout time, with no separate JS-side width value that could disagree with it. `useWindowDimensions` import removed — no longer used anywhere in this file.

**Thirteenth follow-up, same day: the twelfth follow-up's `left: '50%'` fix made it dramatically worse (the bar rendered mostly off-screen to the left, only its last 1-2 tabs visible) — abandoned manual position math entirely rather than guess a third variant of it.** Two different position-math techniques (`useWindowDimensions()`-computed `left`, then `left: '50%'` + negative margin) both failed differently in this exact spot, which is a strong signal the *technique* — computing an absolute `left` for a narrowed `position: 'absolute'` view nested inside this tab navigator — is unreliable here for a reason never fully diagnosed, not that either specific formula was slightly wrong. Rebuilt the whole width/centering approach on ordinary flexbox instead, which needs no assumption about this view's actual resolved width at all:
- `tabBarStyle` goes back to the plain `left: 0, right: 0` box that worked reliably throughout every earlier follow-up (only ever *narrowing* it broke centering) — now fully transparent, with `flexDirection: 'row', justifyContent: 'center'` and no visible fill/shadow/radius of its own.
- The visible pill moved into `tabBarBackground` (`FloatingTabBarBackground`, reintroduced — it existed before blur was added and removed, this is a new implementation): a `{flex:1, alignItems:'center', justifyContent:'center'}` wrapper (filling whatever bounds React Navigation gives it, i.e. the full-width box above) containing one fixed `TAB_BAR_WIDTH`-wide child carrying the actual background/shadow/radius — centered by ordinary flex alignment, not position math.
- `tabBarItemStyle` changed from implicit equal-flex-share sizing to explicit `flex: 0, width: TAB_BAR_WIDTH / 4` per tab — the four items' widths sum to exactly `TAB_BAR_WIDTH`, so `tabBarStyle`'s `justifyContent: 'center'` centers the (now content-sized) icon row directly on top of the identically-centered pill, with neither side needing to reference the outer box's real width.

Flex centering of a fixed-size child inside a parent of unknown width is standard, extensively-tested RN/Yoga behavior — unlike manual absolute-position arithmetic, which has now produced two different failure modes in the same spot. **Lesson**: when a class of fix (not just a specific value) fails twice with different, hard-to-explain symptoms, stop varying the formula and switch to a fundamentally more reliable layout primitive instead of attempting a third variant of the same technique.

**Fourteenth follow-up, same day: the thirteenth follow-up's own flexbox-centering plan (still relying on `tabBarStyle`/`tabBarItemStyle`/`tabBarBackground`) rendered flush left again — abandoned that whole API surface for a fully custom `tabBar`.** Ordinary flex centering was the right *concept*, but it was still expressed through `<Tabs>`'s `screenOptions`, which react-navigation applies to some inner tab-button-row wrapper this app has no visibility into or control over — most likely that inner row doesn't inherit `justifyContent` from `tabBarStyle` at all, meaning the "centering" fix in the thirteenth follow-up was silently never reaching the thing it needed to affect. Three consecutive follow-ups (11th, 12th, 13th) all tried to solve this by configuring that same props surface differently; none worked, which is the same "stop varying the technique" signal as the lesson directly above, one level up — the technique that needed abandoning wasn't "manual position math" specifically, it was "configuring react-navigation's built-in tab bar via its style props" as a whole.

Replaced entirely with a custom `tabBar` render prop on `<Tabs>` (`CustomTabBar`) — one component that renders the whole bar directly: a `position: 'absolute'` wrapper (`pointerEvents="box-none"`, so its full-width invisible span never blocks touches on content beneath it) with `alignItems: 'center'`, containing one fixed-width `View` (background + shadow + radius) that itself lays out `Pressable` icons in a row. No react-navigation-internal wrapper is involved in the actual layout at all — every box is one this code renders and controls directly.

This has one real, functional side effect beyond styling, verified before shipping, not assumed: **a custom `tabBar` completely bypasses expo-router's own `href: null` tab-hiding mechanism** (confirmed by reading `expo-router`'s `TabsClient.js` directly — that mechanism works by injecting `tabBarButton`/`tabBarItemStyle: {display:'none'}` into the *default* tab bar's per-screen options, which a fully custom `tabBar` never reads). `CustomTabBar` reimplements the hiding check itself — which is also what makes `barWidthFor()` correct for investisseur (2 visible tabs) and vendeur (3 visible tabs), a case the earlier fixed `TAB_BAR_WIDTH = 4 × ...` constant was already silently wrong for regardless of today's centering bug, since it never accounted for role-based tab hiding at all.

**Fifteenth follow-up, same day: the fourteenth follow-up's own hiding check was reading a value expo-router had already deleted — the always-hidden `caisse` tab rendered as an invisible 5th slot, pushing the last real tab to the far right with a gap before it (reported directly: "one is left at the far right alone sticky there").** The first implementation checked `options.href !== null` to decide visibility — but `TabsClient.js`'s href-shortcut transform does `const { href, ...options } = screen.options` before handing options to react-navigation, meaning `href` is *deleted* from what `descriptors[route.key].options` actually contains by the time a custom `tabBar` reads it. Checking a value that's always `undefined` always evaluated to "visible," so `caisse` (and, for investisseur/vendeur, Catalogue/Vendre too) rendered as icon-less slots that still claimed a full share of the row's width — same-shape bug as the eighth/eleventh follow-ups above (verifying a library's behavior by reading only what it *sets*, not tracing what it also *removes* in the same code path). The same transform sets `tabBarItemStyle: { display: 'none' }` on hidden routes, and that field is *not* stripped — checking `descriptors[route.key].options.tabBarItemStyle?.display === 'none'` instead is what expo-router's own default tab bar actually keys its hiding on, so it's guaranteed to stay correct even if a future expo-router version changes how the `href` shortcut itself is implemented internally.

**Sixteenth follow-up, same day: once the architecture (centering, hidden-tab handling) was confirmed correct, scaled the whole design up and dropped the shadow entirely.** Two changes, both requested directly once the underlying layout was proven solid rather than still in question:
- `TAB_CIRCLE_SIZE` 36→**48**, `TAB_ICON_SIZE` 20→**24** (same ~1:2 ratio kept), `FLOATING_TAB_BAR_HEIGHT` 52→**64** (`src/theme/spacing.ts`, same 8px-per-side clearance ratio as the 52/36 pairing). Not a reversal of the earlier "too big" complaints from the eleventh/thirteenth follow-ups — those were about the *bar* growing around a circle that stayed fixed size, making it bulkier without anything else changing; this grows the circle and the bar together, which is a different, requested direction ("now expand it") once "is it centered/sized correctly at all" was no longer in doubt.
- Removed `shadow.lg` from the pill entirely — reported directly as an unwanted rectangle visible behind it. The pill is now just its own flat white (`palette.surfaceElevated`) fill with no shadow, border, or any other layer — nothing renders behind it at all.

**Seventeenth follow-up, same day: the "rectangle behind the pill" complaint persisted after the shadow removal above — turned out to be two separate things, one a stale build and one a real architectural gap.** First, the shadow removal (sixteenth follow-up) hadn't been picked up on-device yet — a reminder to fully relaunch was missed that push, so the old shadowed build was still what was being looked at; confirmed by re-grepping the file for any remaining `shadow` reference (none). Second, once actually looking past that: `Screen.tsx`'s `tab` mode was adding `paddingBottom: FLOATING_TAB_BAR_CLEARANCE` to every tab screen's `SafeAreaView` — same background color as the rest of the page, so not a visually distinct "rectangle," but a real, deliberately reserved dead zone where content was never allowed to render, which is a fundamentally different mechanism from how Robinhood's floating bar actually works: its content extends the *full* screen height and scrolls underneath the bar, which simply overlays whatever's currently there, rather than content stopping short to leave the bar an empty space to sit in. Reported directly, unprompted by any specific visual artifact this time, just the explicit design preference: "I want the page to go across its business... the nav bar just pops out on top... you can scroll and do your stuff."

Removed that `paddingBottom` from `Screen.tsx` entirely — `tab` is now a semantic-only marker (still passed at all 4 tab-screen call sites, still documents "this is one of the tab-bar screens") with no different behavior from a default screen's edges. `FLOATING_TAB_BAR_CLEARANCE` itself is untouched and still used directly by the few genuinely fixed, interactive elements that must never render under the bar regardless of scroll position — `vendre.tsx`'s cart checkout panel, both `vendre.tsx`'s and `catalogue.tsx`'s FABs — only ordinary scrollable page content stopped reserving it.

**Known, accepted residual risk, not silently swept under**: a tab screen whose content is short enough to fit entirely on-screen without scrolling (so there's no scroll gesture available to reveal anything from under the bar) could have its own last interactive element sit partially under the pill with no way to bring it above the bar. `plus.tsx`'s "Se déconnecter" button is the most likely candidate (its `ScrollView` only has `paddingBottom: spacing[10]`, 40px — less than the pill's own footprint). Not preemptively fixed here since it depends on real per-screen content length under specific roles/states, which is exactly the kind of thing to verify by looking at the affected screen rather than guessing a padding number in the abstract — if a specific screen's last element turns out genuinely unreachable, add padding to *that screen's own* `contentContainerStyle`, not back to `Screen.tsx` globally (that would silently reintroduce the reserved-gap-on-every-screen behavior this follow-up just removed).

### Auth flow

`stores/auth.ts` (`useAuthStore`) is a Zustand store initialized in the root `_layout.tsx` via `initialize()`. It calls `supabase.auth.getSession()` and loads the user's profile + memberships into `AppSession`. If the user has exactly one business it auto-selects it; otherwise `(app)/onboarding` prompts selection. The `(app)/_layout.tsx` redirects to `/(welcome)/` when no session.

Authentication is **WhatsApp OTP only** — no email/password. Flow: anonymous Supabase session → `create-phone-verification` Edge Function (sends 6-digit code via WhatsApp Twilio, returns only `verificationId`) → user types code in-app via `OtpInput` → `verify-phone-code` Edge Function verifies code and marks row `verifie` → for login: `restore-phone-session` generates magic link → client calls `verifyOtp`; for register: `upgradePhone` sets profile phone + lifts anonymous flag.

iOS AutoFill: `textContentType="oneTimeCode"` on the hidden `OtpInput` TextInput causes iOS to suggest the OTP from recent WhatsApp notifications above the keyboard.

Creating a business generates the UUID client-side first, then inserts — this avoids an RLS race where `SELECT` after `INSERT ... RETURNING` fires before the `handle_business_created` trigger creates the membership row.

**Lost/stolen-phone session revocation (`stores/auth.ts`'s `revokeOtherSessions()`, added 2026-09-27, security audit checklist 1.15).** One button, Paramètres → Sécurité → "Déconnecter tous les autres appareils" — calls `supabase.auth.signOut({ scope: 'others' })` directly, no new table, no per-device list. Deliberately not the more elaborate design first proposed (a `user_sessions` registry with a heartbeat + Realtime revocation flag, mirroring the biometric lock's soft-flag pattern) — overruled as over-engineered for this app's actual scale: nobody runs multiple simultaneous devices as a normal pattern yet, so "kill everything else" matches what a worried vendor actually wants to tap, not a session picker. `scope: 'others'` (not the default `'global'`) is the one that matters: it revokes every *other* refresh token server-side while leaving the device that tapped the button fully logged in — matches this feature's whole point (fix the problem from the phone you're holding without also locking yourself out) and the checklist's own literal test shape ("device A stays logged in, device B gets revoked"). Two things worth knowing if this is ever touched again: `others` scope fires no `SIGNED_OUT` event (so the current device's own auth-state listener is correctly untouched), and this revokes the *refresh* token only — a revoked device's already-issued *access* token JWT keeps working until it naturally expires (up to the project's 3600s lifetime), since that's an inherent JWT property, not something `signOut` can retroactively undo. Verified against real GoTrue behavior, not mocked — `__tests__/integration/session-revocation.integration.test.ts` signs the same test user in on two independent client instances, revokes from one, and asserts the other's `refreshSession()` call fails while the revoking client's own session stays valid.

Joining a business uses invite codes (`equipe` store generates/revokes them) via the `join_business()` SECURITY DEFINER RPC — this is the **only** way to insert a membership row (the open INSERT policy was dropped in v43). Invite code generation uses `Math.random()` — **do not use `crypto.getRandomValues()`**, it is not available in Hermes (React Native's JS engine).

**Signup flow polish, `(welcome)/creer.tsx` (2026-09-27), prompted by an external UX research memo — most of what it asked for was already built, three gaps were real.** The memo assumed this flow was a from-scratch rebuild candidate; reading the actual code first found the +1-country-code bug already fixed (`src/lib/countries.ts` hardcodes `'GN'` specifically to kill it), OTP auto-submit-on-complete already built (`OtpInput`'s `onComplete` fires the instant 6 digits are entered), Android's no-permission-prompt SMS auto-read already built (`modules/sms-retriever/`), and the signup/login fork already mostly collapsed (`creer.tsx` is phone-first; an existing number shows "Ce numéro a déjà un compte" → "Se connecter" with the phone carried over, not a blind either/or choice). Three things were genuinely missing, now fixed:
- **No progress indicator at all.** Added a 3-segment bar + "Étape X sur 3" text, driven by `STEP_ORDER` (`['phone','otp','details']`) and the step's own index — never renders at 0/3, since step 1 of 3 fills its own segment rather than starting from an empty bar (the field-tested "pre-stamp what's already true" point the memo made, which does apply here even though most of the memo's other claims didn't).
- **WhatsApp support only lived on the landing screen (`(welcome)/index.tsx`), not inside the signup flow itself.** Extracted the landing screen's local `SUPPORT_WA_URL` + `Linking.openURL(...).catch(() => {})` into a shared `openSupportChat()` (`src/utils/whatsapp.ts`) so the number/greeting can't drift between the two call sites the way this codebase's copy has drifted elsewhere, and added the same corner affordance to `creer.tsx`, rendered outside the 3 step branches so it survives phone/OTP/business-name alike. Labeled "Aide," not "WhatsApp," on this screen specifically — `creer.tsx`'s OTP step already has its own "Ouvrir WhatsApp" button (opens the WhatsApp app to find the OTP message, a different purpose, via the pre-existing `openWhatsApp()` in the same file) — reusing "WhatsApp" as the corner label too would put two different-purpose affordances with the same name on one screen.
- **`BusinessDetailsStep`'s submit button was still "Créer mon commerce"** — the memo's own premise that a `créer`→`ajouter` copy pass had already happened and just needed re-applying was checked against the codebase's actual history and found false; no such sweep exists here. Rather than re-guess a default, `submitLabel` was changed from an optional prop defaulting to `'Créer mon commerce'` to a **required** prop — both real call sites (`(welcome)/creer.tsx`'s fresh-signup path, now `'Ouvrir mon commerce'`; `(app)/onboarding/creer.tsx`'s already-verified-user-adding-a-business path, already `'Créer le commerce'`) now state their own copy explicitly, so a future third caller can't silently inherit a stale default the way this one did. Two other, unrelated "Créer mon commerce" buttons (`(welcome)/index.tsx`'s landing CTA, `(app)/acces-supprime.tsx`, `DemoBanner.tsx`) were deliberately left alone — they're entry CTAs expressing intent to start the flow, not the flow's own final submit button, and read fine as-is.

### App Store review demo account

`supabase/functions/create-phone-verification/index.ts` checks for a `DEMO_PHONE` environment variable (set in Supabase Edge Function secrets). If the phone matches, the row is inserted with token `000000` and status `en_attente` — no WhatsApp message is sent. The reviewer enters `000000` in the OTP input to proceed. This lets Apple reviewers log in without WhatsApp access. The `DEMO_PHONE` value is `+15555555555`.

### WhatsApp OTP Android autofill (`modules/sms-retriever/`)

Android equivalent of the iOS `textContentType="oneTimeCode"` autofill above — but iOS gets that for free from the OS, while Android needs actual native code, because WhatsApp's "one-tap"/"zero-tap" authentication-template autofill button delivers the code via the same on-device broadcast Google's **SMS Retriever API** listens for (`com.google.android.gms.auth.api.phone.SmsRetriever`, action `SMS_RETRIEVED_ACTION`) — it is not a real carrier SMS, WhatsApp itself fires the broadcast, but the receiving mechanism is identical. That's also why the 11-character "app signature hash" WhatsApp Business Manager asks for is computed with the exact same algorithm as Google's `AppSignatureHelper` (`base64(sha256(packageName + " " + hexCert))[:9 bytes]`) — it has to match whatever certificate actually signs the APK on the user's device. Since Play App Signing is enabled for Patron, that's the **App signing key certificate** shown in Play Console → Protected with Play → Play Store protection → "Protect app signing key" → Manage Play app signing (not the upload key below it, and not derivable from the SHA-1/SHA-256 fingerprints shown there — those are hashes *of* the cert, not the raw cert bytes the algorithm needs; the actual bytes have to come from a real signed APK, e.g. via Play Console's App bundle explorer → Downloads → Signed, universal APK).

`modules/sms-retriever/` is a local Expo Module (Android-only per `expo-module.config.json`) wrapping `SmsRetrieverClient`. `OtpInput`'s `whatsappAutofill` prop (only set on the four screens where the code actually arrives via WhatsApp: `connexion.tsx`, `creer.tsx`, `rejoindre.tsx`, `milestone/phone.tsx` — deliberately not on `recuperation.tsx`, which is email-based) starts the retriever and auto-fills on a matching broadcast. This hash is a **one-time registration**, not per-build: Google holds the App signing key permanently and re-signs every future release with it, so new version codes/EAS builds never require re-registering with WhatsApp — only an explicit "Upgrade your app signing key" action in Play Console would invalidate it. The native receiver itself, however, only ships in the app once a version built after this module was added goes out — an OTA `eas update` alone will not activate it, since it's native (Kotlin) code, not JS.

**`modules/sms-retriever/android/build.gradle` must use the declarative `expo-module-gradle-plugin`, not the legacy `applyKotlinExpoModulesCorePlugin()` pattern (fixed 2026-07-14).** The module was originally authored with the old `apply from: ExpoModulesCorePlugin.gradle` + `applyKotlinExpoModulesCorePlugin()` idiom, which used to inject `compileSdk`/`minSdk`/Kotlin from the root project. Under Expo SDK 54 that mechanism no longer sets `compileSdk`, and SDK 54's Android Gradle Plugin makes `compileSdk` **mandatory with no fallback** — so the first production Android build after the module was added failed in the `Run gradlew` phase with `project ':sms-retriever' does not specify` `compileSdk`` (and a cascading duplicate on `async-storage`, since the broken module aborted the RN gradle plugin's per-module `compileSdk` injection loop mid-pass). Surfaced only as the generic `EAS_BUILD_UNKNOWN_GRADLE_ERROR` — the real cause was in the log tail. Fix: match the modern pattern every bundled Expo module now uses (`plugins { id 'com.android.library'; id 'expo-module-gradle-plugin' }` — see `node_modules/expo-local-authentication/android/build.gradle`), which pulls SDK versions from the root project so nothing is hardcoded to drift. iOS is unaffected (module is Android-only).

### Biometric-only lock (`lib/lock.ts` + `app/(auth)/verrouille.tsx`)

There is **no PIN anywhere in the app** (removed in migration_v131, which also dropped `profiles.pin_hash`/`pin_updated_at` and the pre-existing dead `memberships.pin_hash` column). Re-entry after any lock is Face ID/Touch ID only, falling back to a full WhatsApp OTP re-login when biometric is unavailable, unenrolled, or hard-fails — this was a deliberate product decision, confirmed explicitly: even though it means a non-biometric device hits a full OTP round trip on *every* qualifying lock (not just a killed-and-relaunched app), not just occasionally.

One mechanism handles both triggers that used to be two separate, inconsistent ones (a route-based PIN screen for cold start, and a separate in-place blur overlay for backgrounding — the latter used to fail *open*, silently granting access, when no biometric was enrolled; that gap is closed by having only one path, which never fails open):
- **Cold start**: `useAuthStore.locked`, checked via `isLocked()` at the very start of `initialize()` before any session hydration — a killed-and-relaunched app lands on `/(auth)/verrouille` instead of silently opening back inside.
- **Background-return**: folded into the existing foreground-sync `AppState` listener in `app/(app)/_layout.tsx` (not a separate listener) — tracks how long the app was backgrounded in a ref, and if it was ≥ `BACKGROUND_MS` (5 minutes) on return to `'active'`, calls `lock()` and skips that tick's `refreshActiveBusiness()`/`trySync()` since a redirect is about to unmount the tree anyway.

Both routes converge on the same `lock()`/`verrouille.tsx` pair:
- `lock()` (from Paramètres → "Verrouiller", or the background timer above) sets a soft-lock flag via `setLocked(true)` — it deliberately does **not** touch the Supabase session, refresh token, or any domain store. That's what lets `unlockWithBiometric()` restore the session for free (via `loginWithBiometric()`) with no WhatsApp OTP. `logout()` remains the only path that wipes everything, for signing out / switching accounts — now also the only fallback when biometric itself is unavailable, since there's no PIN.
- `unlockWithBiometric()` (`stores/auth.ts`) returns one of `'unlocked' | 'retryable' | 'unavailable' | 'restore-failed'`, not a bare boolean — collapsing every failure into one boolean would make an accidental cancel or an interrupted Face ID prompt indistinguishable from "no biometric hardware at all," forcing an unnecessary full sign-out. `'retryable'` (cancel, interruption, a single bad read) offers an immediate re-prompt from `verrouille.tsx`; only `'unavailable'` (no hardware/enrollment, or a hard failure like `lockout`/`passcode_not_set`) degrades to a full OTP re-login. `'restore-failed'` means biometric succeeded but `loginWithBiometric()` couldn't refresh the session (e.g. offline) — this must never be treated as a failed auth attempt or forced into a sign-out, since the user did nothing wrong.
- `authenticateAsync`'s `disableDeviceFallback` was originally set to `true` — the SDK defaults this to `false`, which lets the OS's own device-passcode prompt stand in for biometric. **Reversed back to `false` 2026-09-26 — see the dated follow-up at the end of this section for why.**
- A module-level `_biometricPromptInFlight` guard in `unlockWithBiometric()` drops a second call outright while one is still pending, instead of letting it reach `authenticateAsync` again — `expo-local-authentication` supports exactly one in-flight prompt per app, so a stacked call could in principle be silently rejected by the OS with no UI. In practice this wasn't the cause of the bug below, but it's a real race worth guarding regardless. The mount-time auto-attempt in `verrouille.tsx` is also deferred with `InteractionManager.runAfterInteractions` so it doesn't fire mid-route-transition. Failures log the real `result.error` to Sentry (`biometric_authenticate_failed`/`_threw`) since collapsing straight to `'retryable'`/`'unavailable'` hides the actual native reason — this is what diagnosed the bug below.
- **Tapping "Réessayer" silently did nothing on a real device — no Face ID sheet, no error, nothing.** Root cause, found by temporarily surfacing `result.error` on-screen: `missing_usage_description`. `app.json`'s `ios.infoPlist.NSFaceIDUsageDescription` was correct and had been for a long time, but Info.plist values are baked into the app at **native build time** — a JS reload or an `eas update` OTA push can never deliver them, since OTA only ships the JS bundle over the existing native shell. The device had a native binary installed from before this key made it into an actual `eas build`. No code fix exists for this class of bug; the only remedy is a fresh `eas build` (matching whatever profile/distribution the test device uses) followed by reinstalling that build, not another OTA update. Same category as the SMS-retriever native module documented above — always check "was this delivered by an OTA update or a real native build?" before debugging any permission/native-module symptom that reads as "nothing happens at all."
- **Intermittent "nothing happens" on both iOS and Android (fixed 2026-07-14), a second distinct cause from the one above.** User reports and a side-by-side comparison against Robinhood's lock screen (whose native biometric sheet reliably appears every time) showed the app landing straight on the `'retry'` fallback screen with no native prompt ever shown — `InteractionManager.runAfterInteractions` in `verrouille.tsx` has no timeout, so a stuck/never-resolving interaction handle from the route transition could delay `attemptBiometric()` indefinitely with zero visible sign anything was wrong. Fixed by racing it against a flat `setTimeout(fire, 600)`, guarded by a `fired` flag so whichever fires first wins and the other is a no-op. Also: the `'checking'` phase previously showed only the lock icon + "Se connecter via WhatsApp" — no manual retry button — so a stalled silent attempt stranded the user with only the full-logout escape hatch. "Réessayer" is now rendered in both `'checking'` and `'retry'`; tapping it while an attempt is genuinely still in flight is safe, since `_biometricPromptInFlight` just resolves the extra call to `'retryable'` instead of firing a second native prompt. Neither fix is platform-specific — `verrouille.tsx` and `unlockWithBiometric()` have no `Platform.OS` branches, so both apply identically to iOS and Android; the only intentional platform difference in this flow is `disableDeviceFallback: true`, which is meaningful on iOS only (Android's `BiometricPrompt` has no PIN-fallback behavior to disable in the first place).
- **Android-only: real fingerprint success still landing on the `'retry'` screen, a third distinct cause (found 2026-07-17 via a production Sentry event, mitigated same day — not conclusively fixed, since it's a probabilistic OS race).** A user reported the fingerprint prompt visibly succeeding yet still seeing the `'retry'` screen; the corresponding Sentry `biometric_authenticate_failed` event showed `error: 'user_cancel'` (not in `HARD_FAIL`, so it correctly soft-failed to `'retryable'` — the code did what it was designed to do, but the "cancel = the user actually dismissed it" assumption didn't hold) on a Samsung Galaxy A14 5G, Android 15, `device.class: 'low'`, 985MB free memory at the time. Root cause: `app/(app)/_layout.tsx`'s background-return `AppState` listener calls `lock()` the instant `AppState` reports `'active'`, but Android's own window-focus restoration after returning from background runs on a native timeline entirely separate from `verrouille.tsx`'s `InteractionManager`/timeout guard (which only protects against the *in-app* React Navigation transition, per the fix directly above) — on a low-end/low-memory device that native focus restoration can still be settling when the 600ms fallback fires `authenticateAsync`, and `BiometricPrompt` auto-cancels if invoked before the window actually has focus, surfacing as `user_cancel` indistinguishable from a real dismissal.
