The rate limit is defense-in-depth on top of that entropy, not the thing actually keeping codes safe.
- **`join_business()` returns distinct errors for expired/used/invalid** instead of one generic message — a deliberate product decision from this codebase's own history (`migration_v46.sql`), made specifically to help a real user who mistyped a code or whose code lapsed overnight, rather than leaving them with an unhelpful catch-all. Accepted for the same entropy reason as above: an attacker can't productively exploit which specific error comes back, since finding a code close enough to real to make the distinction observable is already the infeasible part.

**Fuzz-testing `submit_sale` (checklist 1.8, `migration_v187.sql`, 2026-09-27) found two real bugs — worth recording *how* they were found, since reading the code alone hadn't caught either one across many prior sessions of work on this function.** The pattern each time: predict a concrete adversarial input, write an integration test asserting the *correct* behavior, run it against the live (unfixed) function first to see it actually fail, only then write the fix. Both predictions came from re-reading the function's own guard clauses literally rather than trusting what they were obviously *intended* to do:
- **Negative quantity silently added stock.** The stock guard on both deduction `UPDATE`s was `stock_qty >= qty` — with a negative `qty`, that's true for any non-negative `stock_qty`, so it never blocked anything, and `stock_qty - (-5)` is `stock_qty + 5`. Confirmed live: a cart with `qty: -5` against a product at `stock_qty` 10 left it at 15, while a real (negative-total) sale still got recorded.
- **Cross-business `product_id` let one business deduct another's stock.** Neither the cost_price lookups nor the stock-deduction `UPDATE`s anywhere in the function filtered by `business_id` — only `WHERE id = product_id`. Since the function is `SECURITY DEFINER`, RLS provides zero protection here on its own; nothing but the function body's own logic enforces tenant isolation, and it didn't. Confirmed live: business B's own `submit_sale` call, referencing business A's `product_id`, deducted 10 units from business A's stock (50 → 40) with no error at all — a real cross-tenant IDOR that had been sitting in the single highest-traffic write in the app.

Fixed with an upfront pass validating the *entire* cart before any insert happens (non-empty, every qty positive, every product/variant actually owned by `p_business_id`), plus the same `business_id` filter added directly to the cost_price lookups and stock UPDATEs as defense-in-depth — so a future edit that weakens the upfront pass doesn't silently reopen either bug on its own. Also confirmed, empirically, **not** to be problems (documented so they aren't re-investigated later): duplicate cart lines for the same product correctly sum their deductions; a line missing `unit_price` entirely is already rejected cleanly by `so_lines`' own `NOT NULL` constraint with nothing orphaned; a `product_id` that doesn't exist at all was already caught by the pre-existing "Stock insuffisant" guard (a zero-row `UPDATE` match), also rolled back cleanly. `__tests__/integration/submit-sale-edge-cases.integration.test.ts` covers all of this — both the two fixed bugs and the three confirmed-safe cases — against a real Postgres instance.

**Sweeping every SECURITY DEFINER RPC touching stock or money for the same pattern (`migration_v188.sql`, 2026-09-27) — a direct follow-up to the `submit_sale` fuzz pass above, since that pass was explicitly scoped to one function and the bug class it found had no reason to be unique to it.** Every remaining stock/money-moving definer function was read, then live-tested against the unfixed function with a real cross-business call before any fix was written — same "predict → test live → confirm → fix → retest" discipline as `submit_sale`, and for good reason: **one of the six real bugs this pass found was NOT predicted by reading at all.**

- **`confirm_reception()`** — an existing `product_id` in `p_lines` was never checked against `p_business_id` before either overwriting its `sale_price` directly, or inserting a `po_lines` row referencing it. A caller could overwrite another business's product price outright, or splice a foreign product/variant into their own purchase order.
- **`create_purchase_order()`** — the identical gap: `po_lines` was inserted straight from `p_lines`' `product_id`/`variant_id` with zero ownership check.
- **`receive_purchase_order()`** — the variant-stock AVCO path (`product_variants` lookup + `UPDATE`) never constrained `business_id`, inconsistent with the parallel plain-product path in the *same function*, which already did. This one only mattered as defense-in-depth once the two bugs above were fixed (nothing should be able to get a foreign `variant_id` into `po_lines` anymore) — confirmed still live and exploitable on unfixed code via a directly-inserted `po_lines` row bypassing both upstream checks.
- **`cancel_sale()`** — the stock-restore loop's `UPDATE products`/`product_variants` had the same missing filter. Lower real-world severity (the ids come from `so_lines` of an already business-scoped sale, so exploiting this today would require first exploiting something else to get a foreign id into `so_lines`), but fixed the same way for the same reason: a future edit shouldn't be able to silently reopen this just because nothing currently reaches it.
- **`create_product_with_stock()`** — `p_stock_move`'s `business_id` and `created_by` were read straight from the client-supplied jsonb instead of being forced to the already-validated `v_business_id`/`auth.uid()`, letting a caller write a `stock_moves` row under an arbitrary business id and attribute it to an arbitrary user.
- **`upsert_product_variants()` — the one this pass would have shipped as "safe" without the live test.** Its archive branch and its final `UPDATE products` both correctly scoped by `AND business_id = p_business_id`, so a foreign `p_product_id` made those two harmless no-ops — reading the code, that looked like the whole function was safe. But the `INSERT INTO product_variants` branch had no ownership check of its own: it happily created a real variant row stamped with the *caller's* `business_id` and the *victim's* `product_id` — a row that shouldn't be able to exist at all. The first version of this migration's own header comment listed this function under "proven safe" until the actual test run against unfixed code came back with a variant sitting on the victim's product. Left as-is, this would have been the exact same mistake `submit_sale` already made once: trusting that a function *looks* safe because most of its paths are scoped, without empirically checking the one that isn't.

**Read and live-tested as already safe, no change needed**: `edit_sale`, `record_client_payment`, `pay_supplier_debt`, `record_injection`, `edit_injection`, `record_withdrawal`, `request_payout`, `confirm_payout`, `attach_transaction_proof`, `delete_transaction_proof`. Each is safe for one of two reasons, not by accident: either it jointly scopes a lookup by `business_id` *and* the object id in the same `WHERE` clause (so a foreign id just matches zero rows), or it takes no separate `business_id` parameter at all and instead derives the authorization-relevant business id *from the target row itself* (`SELECT business_id INTO v_business_id FROM table WHERE id = p_id`) — safe because there's no independently-suppliable `business_id` for that derived value to disagree with.

**A real but much lower-severity aside, noted and deliberately not fixed**: `submit_sale`, `submit_carnet_debt`, and `edit_sale` all accept a client-supplied `p_client_id` and insert/compare it with no check that it belongs to the caller's own business. Unlike every bug above, this one isn't actually reachable cross-business in practice: every real consumer of `sale_orders.client_id` (the client ledger screen, `fetchSales`) already scopes its own query by `business_id` first, and RLS independently blocks reading a foreign business's `clients` row regardless of the FK. A foreign `client_id` written this way just sits inert — never read back by anyone who doesn't already own that business. Flagged rather than fixed because fixing it would mean re-touching three already-hardened functions for a gap with no demonstrated exploit path, not because it wasn't looked at.

Also found and fixed as incidental cleanup while testing `receive_purchase_order`: a stale, unreachable 2-arg `receive_purchase_order(uuid, uuid)` overload, left over from before `confirm_reception` existed (`migration_v45.sql`/`v48.sql` created it; every later signature change correctly `DROP FUNCTION`ed its own immediately-prior overload — `v50`, `v65`, `v83`, `v88` all do — but none of them ever went back for this original one, since by the time each of those shipped it was no longer the "immediately prior" signature). Harmless in production (the only remaining direct caller, `confirm_reception`'s internal `PERFORM`, always passes all 5 positional args, which is never ambiguous) — but it broke a test written for this sweep with a PostgREST "Could not choose the best candidate function" overload-ambiguity error, the same class of leftover-overload bug already documented under "Image messages" (`migration_v132.sql`) and `submit_carnet_debt` (`migration_v178.sql`). Dropped in `migration_v188.sql`.

All six real fixes plus all ten safe-confirmations live in `__tests__/integration/cross-business-po-flow.integration.test.ts`, `cross-business-money-ops.integration.test.ts`, and `cross-business-proof-attachment.integration.test.ts` — every one of them a live cross-business RPC call against a real Postgres instance, run against the unfixed function first to confirm the bug was real before writing the fix, exactly like `submit_sale`'s pass above.

**Standing rule, going forward: every new SECURITY DEFINER function that takes an object id must ship with a live cross-business integration test proving the lookup is scoped to the caller's own business — not a code-review judgment call that the `WHERE` clause "looks right."** This sweep exists because `submit_sale` was read by multiple people across many sessions and still had a live cross-tenant IDOR; `upsert_product_variants` was *specifically read for this exact bug class, in this exact sweep,* and still got misjudged safe until the test ran. Reading catches the pattern you're already looking for; it reliably misses the one file where the same pattern shows up in a shape you weren't scanning for (an `INSERT` instead of an `UPDATE`, a second, unscoped branch alongside two correctly-scoped ones). A function that receives a client-supplied id and does anything with it inside a definer body is not done until a test has tried calling it from a different business and confirmed it gets rejected — that's what keeps this bug class dead, not another reading pass.

### Integration tests (`__tests__/integration/`)

The Jest suite in `__tests__/` mocks `supabase.rpc` entirely — it verifies the client calls the RPC with the right params, not that the SQL function itself is correct. `__tests__/integration/` closes that gap: it runs `submit_sale` / `cancel_sale` / `edit_sale` / `join_business` for real, against a local Supabase stack (`supabase start`), asserting on actual stock/payment/membership rows.

- **Local Postgres via Colima, not Docker Desktop** — this machine's macOS predates Sonoma, which Docker Desktop's cask requires. `brew install colima docker` gives a CLI-only Docker daemon that works fine for `supabase start`.
- **`[analytics]` is disabled in `supabase/config.toml`** — the vector/logflare log-shipping container bind-mounts the Docker socket in a way Colima's VM doesn't support (`mkdir ...docker.sock: operation not supported`). Not needed for tests, only the Studio log viewer.
- **`scripts/test-db-setup.js`** resets the `public` schema, re-grants the standard Supabase role privileges (`anon`/`authenticated`/`service_role` — lost when the schema is dropped and recreated, since `supabase start` doesn't redo cluster-init grants on an existing volume), then applies `db/schema.sql` followed by every `db/migration_v*.sql` **statement-by-statement** (via `scripts/lib/split-sql.js`, a dollar-quote-aware splitter) rather than one file per query. That granularity matters: Postgres treats a multi-statement simple-query message as a single implicit transaction, so if a file mixes an already-applied statement (schema.sql already has it — full-history replay from empty is something production itself never does) with a genuinely new one, running the file as one query rolls back the new statement too when the old one errors. This bit `migration_v90.sql` specifically — its new `message_type` column was reverted along with an unrelated already-applied policy statement further down the same file, which then made `migration_v91.sql` fail with "column message_type does not exist" one file later. Splitting per-statement fixed it.
- **`__tests__/integration/helpers.ts`** creates real throwaway auth users via the service-role key (`admin.auth.admin.createUser`), signs them in, and calls RPCs through the same `@supabase/supabase-js` client shape the app itself uses — so `auth.uid()` and RLS behave exactly as they do in production, not simulated.
- Run via `npm run test:db:start && npm run test:db:reset && npm run test:integration`. Deliberately excluded from `npm run check` (`jest.config.js`'s `testPathIgnorePatterns`) — it needs Docker running, so it stays a separate, opt-in gate (`jest.integration.config.js`) rather than blocking every fast local iteration.

### Email account recovery

`profiles.recovery_email` (TEXT UNIQUE, nullable) — set proactively by the user in Paramètres as a fallback if they lose their phone number.

**Linking flow (in-app, authenticated):** Paramètres → "Email de récupération" → enter email → `send-email-otp` sends 6-digit code via Resend → user enters code → `link-recovery-email` validates + saves to profile.

**Recovery flow (unauthenticated, on login screen):** "Numéro indisponible ?" → `/(welcome)/recuperation` → same email OTP → `recover-by-email` finds profile by `recovery_email`, generates magic link → `supabase.auth.verifyOtp({ token_hash, type: 'magiclink' })` → session. Identical magic-link pattern to `restore-phone-session`.

**OTP validation pattern in edge functions:** Always fetch the `email_verifications` row by `id` alone first (no status/expiry filters in the query), then check each condition separately with a distinct error message. Combining all filters into one query silently returns null for any failure reason, making debugging impossible.

### Critical: auth store `loading` flag

`(app)/_layout.tsx` renders `null` when `loading === true` — this unmounts the entire navigator, causing a white screen and redirect to `/(welcome)/`. **Never set `loading: true` from a store method called while the user is already inside the app.** 

Auth store has a separate `emailOtpLoading: boolean` flag for email OTP operations (`sendEmailOtp`, `linkRecoveryEmail`). Any future in-app async operations that need a loading state must use their own flag — not the global `loading`. The global `loading` is only for the initial session bootstrap.

**Offline vs. genuinely-invalid session on `initialize()`:** when `supabase.auth.getSession()` returns no session, the code checks `isAuthRetryableFetchError(sessionError)` before deciding what to do with an already-rendered cached session. A retryable fetch error means the device simply couldn't reach the server — the account's real validity is unknown, so the cached session keeps being trusted for offline use. Any other outcome (no error, or a non-retryable auth error like a revoked/invalid refresh token) means the account is genuinely no longer valid server-side, and the cached session must be cleared — it must not keep being trusted indefinitely just because it exists locally.

**`createBusiness()` had no try/catch around its RPC call — the last step of onboarding could hang forever on a bad connection with no error shown (found 2026-08-24, while investigating why 19 real phone-verified users never finished onboarding).** `lib/supabase.ts`'s global `fetchWithTimeout` aborts any request after 15s, but an abort is a **thrown** rejection, not a returned `{data,error}` pair — `createBusiness()`'s bare `await supabase.rpc('create_business_with_membership', ...)` had nothing to catch it, so a timeout (or any other network exception) left `loading: true` set forever, with `error` never populated. The visible symptom: tap "Créer mon commerce" on a slow connection, the button spins, and it never stops — no error, no retry prompt, nothing to screenshot. Since this is the literal last tap of onboarding (phone already verified, the hard part already done), a stuck spinner here reads as a dead app, not a network hiccup, and gives someone no reason to try again. Exactly the same bug class already found and fixed in `stores/investor.ts`'s `fetchBalance`/`fetchPayouts` and `stores/sales.ts`'s `submitCarnetDebt` (see "Offline queue" above) — a bare `await supabase.rpc(...)` with no wrapping `try/catch`. It slipped past the automated `findUnprotectedFetchViolations()` sweep because that check only flags functions that already have `isNetworkError(` handling but forgot `withTimeout(`; `createBusiness()` had no error handling shape at all, so the heuristic never matched it — a reminder that the sweep catches one specific bug shape, not "any unhandled Supabase call." Fixed by wrapping the whole RPC-and-onward body in try/catch, same shape as the sibling `joinBusiness()` (which already had this right). `joinBusiness()` itself was unaffected — already correctly wrapped.

### Supabase client (`lib/supabase.ts`)

Uses `expo-secure-store` for session storage with custom 2 KB chunking (SecureStore has a per-key size limit, so tokens are split across multiple keys). The `@/` alias maps to the project root (configured in `tsconfig.json`).

`lib/supabase.web.ts` is a stub for the web bundle (no-op — the app has no real web target).

### Edge Functions (`supabase/functions/`)

| Function | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `create-phone-verification` | Creates OTP row, sends WhatsApp via Twilio. Bypasses for `DEMO_PHONE` env var (App Store review). Rate-limited via `phone_verification_attempts`. |
| `whatsapp-inbound-webhook` | Marks verification row as `verifie` when user sends token back via WhatsApp |
| `restore-phone-session` | Generates magic link for verified phone, returns `token_hash` for `verifyOtp` |
| `send-whatsapp-otp` | Twilio WhatsApp message dispatch |
| `verify-phone-code` | Verifies 6-digit code entered in-app against `phone_verifications` row; marks `verifie` on match |
| `verify-phone-otp` | OTP verification helper |
| `send-email-otp` | Sends 6-digit recovery code via Resend (`noreply@patron.kolilink.com`). No auth required — user may be locked out. Rate-limited via `email_verification_attempts`. |
| `link-recovery-email` | Validates email OTP then links `recovery_email` to authenticated user's profile. Rejects if email already taken by another account. |
| `recover-by-email` | Validates email OTP, finds profile by `recovery_email`, generates magic link session. No auth required. |
| `send-reconciliation-report` | Standalone reconciliation email — same checks as `send-report-email`'s `include_reconciliation` path, via the shared `supabase/functions/_shared/reconciliation.ts` module. No longer on a cron schedule as of v123; left deployed for manual/debug re-runs (e.g. re-checking right after a fix). Deployed with `--no-verify-jwt` (authenticates via its own `x-cron-secret` header instead of a Supabase JWT). See "Nightly reconciliation" below. |
| `send-report-email` | Sends the founder's one combined daily report (financial summary + reconciliation when `include_reconciliation: true`). Triggered by the "Patron — Rapport Quotidien" cloud routine at 6:00 AM ET. Shares `run_reconciliation()` / `run_display_checks()` / `refresh_reconciliation_run()` / `get_financial_snapshot()` logic with `send-reconciliation-report` via `_shared/reconciliation.ts` so the check logic lives in exactly one place. |
| `dispatch-notification` | Sends Expo push notifications for in-app events (e.g. `sale_completed`). Requires a Supabase JWT (`Authorization: Bearer`); the caller must be a member of `business_id`, **except** for the two partnership-handshake events (`partnership_request`, `partnership_accepted`), authorized via a `business_partnerships` row, and `support_reply`, authorized via the same `profiles.phone` founder check as `is_founder()`. A caller-supplied `target_user_ids` list is intersected against real members of the business server-side — a caller can never target an arbitrary user id. `support_message` recipient resolution bypasses `target_roles`/`target_user_ids` entirely and always routes to `get_founder_id()`, since the founder is never a member of the merchant's business. (Previously unauthenticated and unrestricted — any caller could push to any business/user; fixed alongside the PIN-lock work, no dedicated migration.) |
| `dispatch-chat-notification` | Supabase Database Webhook (not a client-invoked function) on `chat_messages` INSERT — pushes to every other member of the message's business. **Had no authentication check at all until a 2026-07-28 security audit caught it** (the function predates this table — it was never added when it shipped, which is exactly how the gap went unnoticed): the dashboard-side webhook config sends the service_role key as `Authorization: Bearer`, but the function itself never verified that header, so anyone holding the app's public anon key could POST a forged `{ record: { room_id, user_id, sender_name, content } }` directly at its URL and spoof a push (arbitrary sender name + body) to every real member of any business whose room_id they knew — no membership, no real message, no rate limit required. Fixed by checking the Bearer token against `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` with the same `timingSafeEqual` constant-time compare `whatsapp-inbound-webhook` already uses, before trusting `record` at all. **Any new Database Webhook-backed function must verify its own `Authorization` header in code — Supabase's platform-level `verify_jwt` is not a substitute**, since the app's own anon key is itself a validly-signed JWT and would pass that gate trivially. |
| `seed-demo-business` | Called once from the welcome screen's "Essayer" button (`stores/auth.ts`'s `startDemoMode()`) — creates a fake "Boutique Démo" business (5 products, ~12 sales, expenses) for a fresh anonymous session to explore the app pre-signup. **Had no rate limiting until the same 2026-07-28 audit**: `signInAnonymously()` is free and unthrottled, and this function's only gate was "holds a real session" — a scripted loop of anon-signin + call could spam unlimited fake businesses (~50 rows each), inflating storage/cost and polluting founder-facing aggregates (e.g. `get_founder_activity_raw()`, `migration_v156.sql`, which scans all users/sales unwindowed). A per-user idempotency check already existed but doesn't help here, since each scripted call is a brand-new anonymous user with no prior business. Fixed by the same per-IP rate limit `djomi-checkout` already uses — `ip_verification_attempts` (`migration_v113.sql`), `endpoint: 'seed_demo_business'`, capped at 3/hour (this button is only ever pressed once by a real user, so this only bites scripted abuse). |
| `generate-support-draft` | Generates a founder-only AI draft reply for a support conversation (Groq, `llama-3.3-70b-versatile` — free tier, open-weight; see "Support chat" below). Triggered fire-and-forget after every merchant message and on-demand via the founder inbox's "Régénérer". Authorized as either a member of the conversation's business (the merchant-triggered path) or the founder (manual regenerate). Requires the `GROQ_API_KEY` edge function secret. |
| `revenuecat-webhook` | Updates `businesses.subscription_status`/`subscription_expires_at`/`payment_provider`/`revenuecat_customer_id` on RevenueCat subscription events (`INITIAL_PURCHASE`/`RENEWAL`/`UNCANCELLATION`/`PRODUCT_CHANGE`/`EXPIRATION`), and grants the "Inviter un ami" referral bonus on a business's first-ever activation. See "Native IAP paywall" above. Authenticates via a static `Authorization` header (RevenueCat webhooks aren't signature-based like Stripe's), checked against the `REVENUECAT_WEBHOOK_AUTH_HEADER` secret. |
| `send-alpha-quota-reminders` | Cron job (hourly, not yet scheduled — see `migration_v138.sql`): finds every user whose Alpha quota window just reset after being exhausted (`get_and_mark_alpha_quota_resets()`), and pushes a "Vous pouvez parler à Alpha (Pro) maintenant" reminder via `dispatch-notification`'s `alpha_quota_reset` event. Skips entirely 21:00–07:00 UTC (Guinea has no DST, so a flat UTC-hour check stands in for "local night" — see "Alpha" below). Authenticates to `dispatch-notification` itself via the same `x-cron-secret`, since there's no user session to hold a Bearer JWT for a background job. |
| `send-daily-digest` | Cron job (hourly, not yet scheduled — see `migration_v139.sql`): calls `get_and_mark_daily_digest_businesses()` to find every business whose personally-learned send hour is right now, and pushes `dispatch-notification`'s `daily_digest` event (`target_roles: ['administrateur','manager']`, same audience as `sale_completed`) — "La journée est bonne, vous avez fait : X" or "La journée était calme. On se retrouve demain." depending on whether there was revenue today. See "Daily digest" below. |
| `learn-digest-send-hours` | Cron job (once daily, early morning, not yet scheduled — see `migration_v139.sql`): calls `learn_digest_send_hours()` to recompute every business's `digest_send_hour` from their trailing 14-day sales pattern. Kept separate from `send-daily-digest` so the per-business hour isn't relearned on every hourly tick. |
| `djomi-checkout` | Unauthenticated web page (GET serves HTML, POST starts a payment, `?verify=1&ref=...` polls status) for the Alpha Pro subscription via Djomi/Orange Money — see "Djomi out-of-app subscription" below. This is the primary confirmation path (polling), not just the checkout UI. Deployed with `--no-verify-jwt`; never linked from inside the app. |
| `djomi-webhook` | Dormant backstop — re-verifies via Djomi's transaction-status endpoint and activates the paying business's subscription, same as `djomi-checkout`'s poll, but realistically never invoked since the shared Djomi account's one webhook slot points at a different project. Deployed with `--no-verify-jwt`, authenticates via `X-Webhook-Signature` instead of a Supabase JWT. |
| `djomi-sweep` | Cron job, scheduled hourly via `migration_v141.sql`: re-checks any `djomi_pending_payments` row still unresolved after `djomi-checkout`'s poll window gave up (bounded to the last 24h), so a merchant who paid but closed the tab still gets activated. `x-cron-secret`-authenticated, same pattern as `send-alpha-quota-reminders`. |
| `send-activation-reminders` | Cron job, scheduled hourly via `migration_v162.sql`: finds businesses still in their first 24h with neither a product nor a sale (`get_and_mark_activation_reminders()`, `migration_v161.sql`) and pushes `dispatch-notification`'s `activation_nudge_1`/`activation_nudge_2` events (`target_roles: ['administrateur','manager']`) — see "Activation reminders" above. |
| `send-second-action-reminders` | Cron job, scheduled hourly via `migration_v164.sql`: finds businesses that took exactly one activation action and went quiet (`get_and_mark_second_action_reminders()`, `migration_v163.sql`) and pushes `dispatch-notification`'s `second_action_reminder` event (payload `action_type: 'product'\|'sale'\|'debt'`, `target_roles: ['administrateur','manager']`) — see "Second-action reminder" above. |
| `send-revenue-milestone-reminders` | Cron job, scheduled hourly via `migration_v166.sql`: finds GNF businesses whose lifetime revenue just crossed the next threshold in a fixed ladder (`get_and_mark_revenue_milestones()`, `migration_v165.sql`) and pushes `dispatch-notification`'s `revenue_milestone` event (payload `amount`, pre-formatted French-style — `target_roles: ['administrateur','manager']`) — see "Revenue milestone reminder" above. |
| `process-scheduled-account-deletions` | Cron job, scheduled daily (04:00 UTC) via `migration_v171.sql`: finds every `profiles` row whose `pending_deletion_at` (set by `delete_my_account()`, `migration_v170.sql`) has passed and calls `finalize_account_deletion(user_id)` once per row (isolated per-call, not one SQL loop, so one bad row can't abort the batch) — see "Parametres screen" above. |

### Nightly reconciliation (data integrity monitoring)

As of `migration_v123.sql`, there is **one** daily email, not two. The founder's
"Patron — Rapport Quotidien" cloud routine calls `send-report-email` at 6:00 AM ET
with `include_reconciliation: true`, which runs the full check sequence via the
shared `supabase/functions/_shared/reconciliation.ts` module:

1. `run_reconciliation()` — 68 checks across 14 domains (stock, sales,
   payments, COGS, expenses, credit, suppliers, purchase orders, products,
   monetary precision, members, cross-aggregates, temporal, referential
   integrity) + `run_display_checks()` (10 more, covering display-formula
   drift) — writing results to `reconciliation_runs` / `reconciliation_findings`.
2. `get_financial_snapshot()` — an independent revenue/COGS/expenses/net-profit
   recompute straight from the ledger, grouped by currency.
3. Everything is folded into the single combined report emailed via Resend to
   `FOUNDER_EMAIL`. Clean runs send a short "all clear" section; runs with
   findings list each one grouped by severity.

The old standalone `patron-nightly-reconciliation` `pg_cron` job (`0 2 * * *`
UTC → `send-reconciliation-report`) was unscheduled in v123 — that duplicated
coverage the combined report now provides. `send-reconciliation-report` itself
is still deployed (not deleted) for manual/debug re-runs, e.g. re-checking
right after a fix, and still authenticates via its own `x-cron-secret` /
`CRON_SECRET` check rather than a user JWT.

The cron secret lives in **Supabase Vault** (`patron_cron_secret`), pulled at
execution time via `vault.decrypted_secrets` — never stored as plaintext in
`cron.job`.

This system catches data corruption (e.g. an order total that doesn't match
its line items) and, since `run_display_checks()` was added, some
display-formula drift too — but consolidating the dashboard/reports/catalogue
screens' independently-computed "profit" formulas into one canonical backend
function remains a separate, tracked follow-up.

## Keeping this file current

This file is the main reason a fresh session can work on this codebase without re-discovering the same landmines. **Whenever you fix a bug or land a change whose root cause is not obvious from reading the resulting code** — a wrong assumption, a silent regression, a security gap, a platform gotcha, a rewrite that dropped a protection nobody noticed — add an entry before ending the task. Don't wait to be asked.

**What to add it for:**
- A DB migration with a real "why" (not just "adds column X") → a new row in the migration table.
- An app-code-only fix with a non-obvious cause (nothing to do with a migration) → a short note in the relevant architecture section, or a new subsection if it's a new system (see the Biometric-only lock section above for the shape: what it is, why it's structured that way, and the specific failure mode the current code avoids).
- A correction the user gives you mid-session about how this repo specifically works (not a general style preference — those belong in memory, not here) → fold it into the relevant section.

**What NOT to add:** anything a session could get by reading the current code — type shapes, prop lists, a function's current signature. Those entries go stale as the code moves and become actively misleading; the code itself is always the source of truth for "what," this file is for "why" and "what already went wrong here." If you're about to write a sentence that's just a paraphrase of the code you're looking at, stop.

**Format:** match the existing entries — migration table rows are one line: what changed, why, what broke without it. Architecture-section prose follows the pattern in "Biometric-only lock" / "Offline queue" / "Nightly reconciliation" above: state the mechanism, then the specific bug or race it was built to avoid. Keep it as dense as what's already here — this file earns its keep by being denser and more specific than generic docs, not by being long.

When migration numbers or table rows accumulate past what's practical to keep in full, compress older stretches the way the existing "(Note: migration_v58.sql through v75.sql... undocumented here)" line does, rather than deleting the history.
